## Page 1


POTENTIAL HESSIAN ASCENT III:
SAMPLING THE SHERRINGTON–KIRKPATRICK MODEL AT β < 1/2
EWAN DAVIES, HOLDEN LEE, JUSPREET SINGH SANDHU, AND JONATHAN SHI
Abstract. We give a polynomial-time algorithm to sample from the Gibbs measure of the Sherrington–
Kirkpatrick model with negligible total-variation distance (TVD) error up to inverse temperature
β < 1/2. Prior work obtained TVD error guarantees only up to β ≈0.295, while results covering the
entire replica-symmetric regime β < 1 gave guarantees only in Wasserstein distance.
Our approach demonstrates that the same potential Hessian ascent previously developed for opti-
mization also functions as a sampling algorithm by implementing algorithmic stochastic localization
at high temperature. By estimating the covariance of the tilted Gibbs distribution via Gaussian inte-
gration by parts, overlap concentration, and precise cavity estimates, we show that a Hessian-ascent
process achieves an O(1) Wasserstein error guarantee for finite-time localization, improving on the
previous o(n). A careful comparison of stochastic localization with the Hessian ascent process and a
free probability argument controlling the diagonal sub-algebra of the Hessian improves this to O(1)
in KL divergence. We then use Jarzynski’s equality with rejection sampling, along with entropy
contraction on the time-T localized distribution, to refine the error to o(1) in TVD up to a constant
time T and to complete the sampling with the polarized walk.

1. Introduction
We give a polynomial-time algorithm to sample from the Gibbs measure of the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick (SK)
model at inverse temperatures β < 1/2 with total variation error on(1). The (random) Gibbs measure for the
n-dimensional SK model is a probability measure on {−1,1}n given by
µβA(x) =
1
Z(βA)e
1
2 ⟨x,βAx⟩,
where A is a random n × n matrix from the Gaussian orthogonal ensemble and the normalizing constant
Z(βA) =
∑
x∈{−1,1}n
e
1
2 ⟨x,βAx⟩
is the partition function. This means that A is a symmetric matrix whose diagonal entries Ai,i are independently
distributed as N(0,2/n) and whose off-diagonal entries Ai,j = Aj,i for i ≠j are independently distributed
according to N(0,1/n), where N(µ,σ2) is the Gaussian distribution with mean µ and variance σ2. The
parameter β > 0 represents inverse temperature, and with this parametrization, the replica-symmetric (or
high-temperature) regime corresponds to β < 1. It is widely expected that a polynomial-time sampling
algorithm exists throughout the replica-symmetric regime. Our argument establishes this up to β < 1/2.
Theorem 1.1 (Informal version).
Let 0 < β < 1/2. Given any fixed δ > 0, for all n ⩾n0(β,δ), with probability 1 −δ over A, Algorithm 2 gives a
sample from a distribution that is on(1) in TVD from µβA in polynomial time.
1.1. Spin glass models and sampling. The SK model is the canonical example of a spin glass model:
first introduced in statistical physics to study systems with dense, disordered interactions, it has since
become a central object in probability, with a Gibbs measure that exhibits intricate and rich structure,
including replica symmetry breaking at low temperatures [SK75, Par80a, Par80b, Gue01, GT02, Gue03, Tal06,
Tal11a, Tal11b, Pan13a, Pan13b, AC15, CPS23, Sub24]. Interest in spin glass models in computer science
is motivated by their utility as a model of noise in unsupervised learning, signal recovery, or hypothesis
testing problems [MR14, MSS16, Hop18, ALM+21, AST23], their deep relationship to average-case constraint
satisfaction problems [DMS17, Pan18, Gam21, CLSS22, Hua22, JMSS23], and their challenge as a testbed for
algorithms working in high-dimensional and highly non-convex settings [Sub21, Mon19, IS24, SS24, JSS25,
JSS], including noisy quantum algorithms [FGGZ22, BFM+22, BKL+25].
As random Gibbs measures on the hypercube, spin glass models are also a natural testing ground for sampling
algorithms: the target measure is the stationary distribution of natural local Markov chain dynamics, such as
Glauber dynamics, and a central question is whether these dynamics mix rapidly. Average-case guarantees in
these settings are well-motivated, with Bayesian posterior sampling as an example application where the
target distribution is itself random, and with worst-case analyses often failing in regimes where algorithms
actually succeed. The SK model presents a prototypical challenge for sampling from these random measures
induced by dense interactions [BKMR25, Open Problem 15].
One line of work proves functional inequalities for the Gibbs measure or transformations of it [EKZ22, AJK+22],
as this immediately implies fast mixing of the associated Markov chain. For the SK model, this currently
yields high-accuracy total-variation guarantees up to β < 0.295 [AKV24]. These results rely on simple spectral
properties of A, suggesting that further progress requires more fine-grained understanding of the Gibbs
measure and the underlying random matrix.
A complementary line of work is Algorithmic Stochastic Localization (ASL) [AMS22, HMP24]. Stochastic
localization (SL) [Eld13] (equivalently, a diffusion model [Mon25]) is a measure-valued stochastic process that
randomly evolves the original distribution µ0 to progressively more localized distributions µt while maintaining
the Bayesian posterior property that sampling a random µt and then sampling σ ∼µt is equivalent to sampling
from the original distribution σ ∼µ0. ASL is the algorithmization of SL by replacing its computationally
intractable component—namely the magnetization, or equivalently, the score—with efficiently computable
surrogates. Using Approximate Message Passing (AMP), prior work achieves sampling throughout the
replica-symmetric regime β < 1 with o(n) Wasserstein error [AMS22, Cel24]. We improve the error guarantee
to on(1) total variation error when β < 1/2 with a Hessian-based approach.

1.2. Sampling strategy. In SL, the tilt or linear field yt defines µt by the reweighting
µt(σ) ∝exp(⟨σ,yt⟩)µ0(σ),
and the magnetization mt is the average
mt ∶= ∑
σ
σ µt(σ)
of the reweighted/conditional distribution. The evolution of SL can be described in two equivalent ways that
involve mt: the usual stochastic differential equation (SDE) definition is
dyt = mt dt + dBt ,
(SL)
where the mt dt term gradually dominates as t gets large. A change of variables yields an equivalent SDE
that we call Hessian Dynamics1 (HD):
dmt = Cov(µt)dBt .
(HD)
Neither of these two SDEs are algorithmic due to the difficulty of estimating mt from yt or Covσ∼µt(σ) from mt.
But mathematical spin glass theory gives us a third relationship between mt and yt by positing that at all times,
they should jointly extremize an explicit function FTAP(m,y) called the TAP free energy [TAP77, CPS23].
This TAP stationarity principle is only approximately true, but the relationship it specifies is efficiently
computable.
We obtain algorithmic versions of SL and HD by using the TAP stationarity principle to compute surrogates
for the non-algorithmic mt in (SL) and the non-algorithmic covariance Cov(µt) in (HD). The former yields
ASL-TAP and the latter Potential Hessian Dynamics (PHD). Although these arise from the SL and HD
viewpoints respectively, both end up with the same diffusion coefficient for dmt, namely the inverse TAP
Hessian (∇2
mFTAP(mt,yt))−1; they differ only in drift (see §2).
The first major step of the analysis shows that the surrogate covariance (∇2
mFTAP(mt,yt))−1 is within O(1)
of the true covariance Cov(µt) in Frobenius norm distance in expectation. The argument is involved, using
cavity interpolation estimates, overlap concentration, and various delicate symmetry arguments. The result
implies that mt in PHD stays within O(1) Wasserstein distance of HD for constant time, improving on the
o(n) Wasserstein guarantee in [AMS22].
The second step compares ASL-TAP to PHD-TAP to show that yt in ASL-TAP stays within O(1) KL
divergence error of SL for constant time. ASL-TAP and PHD-TAP have the same diffusion terms, and their
drift difference is determined by the diagonal entries of the square of the surrogate covariance, which we
control by a free probability analysis.
Once this O(1) KL divergence error is achieved, we boost it to o(1) using rejection sampling. To accomplish
this, Jarzynski’s equality [Jar97] provides an explicit algorithmic density function by comparing ASL-TAP to
a TAP-based scaffold of explicit density functions.
If we could run ASL to T = ∞, the localized measure would be a point mass. Since our analysis only permits
T = O(1), we stop at a large constant time T = T(β) and sample from µT using an appropriate Markov chain
on the hypercube—the polarized walk—after showing entropy contraction holds for µT at large enough T.
1.3. Technical challenges and contributions. The analysis of our algorithm requires several major
innovations or extensions of techniques beyond what is established in the literature, which we describe here.
Rejection sampling with Jarzynski’s equality (§3). Jarzynski’s equality (JE) is a nonequilibrium
identity from statistical mechanics that yields importance weights for SDE-based sampling [Jar97, AVE24].
We use it to reweight ASL-TAP toward a scaffold of explicit densities, enabling rejection sampling. To our
knowledge, this is the first use of Jarzynski’s equality in a theoretical sampling problem, showing that ASL
can be corrected by reweighting probabilities instead of displacing mass, and it may have broader applicability
in algorithmic contexts.
1The name reflects the general equivalence between the covariance of the Gibbs measure and the Hessian of the free energy with
respect to the linear field [WJ08, Proposition 3.1(a)]. It also reflects that HD belongs to the same Hessian-based formalism as
Potential Hessian Ascent [JSS25, JSS].


## Page 4


POTENTIAL HESSIAN ASCENT III: SAMPLING FOR THE SHERRINGTON–KIRKPATRICK MODEL
3
Covariance estimate from overlap moments (§4). We show that the covariance of the Gibbs distribution
of the planted SK model with plant-dependent linear field on the Nishimori line, and consequently also the
SL-tilted SK model [AMS22], is well-approximated by the inverse of the Hessian of the TAP free energy. The
main difficulty here is that, unlike the well-studied case of the SK model with independent external field, the
tilt yt is itself a random function of the disorder A.
El Alaoui and Gaitonde established this approximation for the standard SK model without plant, tilt, or field
by decomposing the error into functions of overlaps [AG24], then invoking known cavity interpolation [Tal11a,
§1.6] and overlap concentration [Tal11b, §11] estimates. We generalize and extend their work by performing
the decomposition for the planted model with plant-dependent linear field. Since no existing cavity estimates
sufficiently covered the planted model, we also de novo establish various analytic estimates made available by
the cavity interpolation for the planted model (§5).
Regularity of ASL-TAP and PHD (§6). On a high-probability event in A, we show that the coefficients
of ASL-TAP and PHD are uniformly bounded and Lipschitz. These regularity estimates control the SDE
comparison error that goes into the O(1) KL divergence bound, and they also imply concentration of Lipschitz
functionals of the algorithmic trajectory, which is used to control the sample complexity of the Jarzynski
reweighting.
Control of the diagonal through free probability (§B). We use a free probability argument to control
the drift mismatch between ASL-TAP and PHD-TAP, since this mismatch is determined by the diagonal
entries of a squared resolvent matrix. The analysis is similar to a previous characterization for Potential
Hessian Ascent [JSS25], though here the degree of control required is tighter, and singularities may arise
because we cannot afford to regularize with an imaginary component in the resolvent. The new ingredients
are a chaining argument in place of an epsilon net, a cutoff to avoid real-axis singularities, and sharper edge
and derivative estimates for the squared resolvent.
Entropy contraction for localized distributions on wedges (§7). The last technical challenge comes
in sampling from the time-T localized distribution µT for large enough T. For this, we run the polarized walk
restricted to a wedge of the hypercube2. We show that this mixes rapidly by proving that the polarized walk
satisfies entropy contraction with respect to such measures. This is accomplished through the framework
of localization schemes [CE25] by extending and combining Eldan, Kohler and Zeitouni’s [EKZ22] needle
decomposition technique into a 2-stage decomposition, with a bound on the covariance of the decomposed
measures, itself established via a local limit theorem for Bernoulli random variables.
Hessian Ascent for sampling. Potential Hessian Ascent [JSS25] was introduced as an optimization
algorithm for the SK model Hamiltonian at β > 1, with the covariance of the algorithmic increments chosen
based on a resolvent of the Hessian of the generalized TAP free energy [CPS23]. Here we show that at
β < 1/2, the same Hessian-resolvent formalism yields a sampling algorithm: up to a small rank-one correction,
PHD is the limit of PHA as the imaginary component in the resolvent and the step size both go to 0. This
demonstrates that Hessian Ascent is both an optimization algorithm and a sampling algorithm, and in fact
implements a denoising diffusion model [Mon25].
TVD sampling without a functional inequality. Our method achieves on(1) total-variation sampling
under weaker hypotheses than current functional-inequality approaches appear to require. In particular, we
do not assume a high-probability operator-norm bound on the covariance along the localization path [CE25,
HMRW25]—an estimate that is not presently known throughout the replica-symmetric regime—and instead
combine O(1) covariance control with Jarzynski correction and a final localized mixing argument.
1.4. Prior work. There are two main approaches to sampling from spin glass models. One is to prove
functional inequalities (e.g. Poincaré or modified log-Sobolev inequalities), typically via stochastic localization,
and deduce rapid mixing of Glauber or Langevin dynamics. It can be sufficient to show weak functional
inequalities, which essentially establish the efficacy of these dynamics given a warm start or suitable annealing.
2For technical reasons, the polarized walk is more convenient to analyze than Glauber dynamics.


## Page 5


POTENTIAL HESSIAN ASCENT III: SAMPLING FOR THE SHERRINGTON–KIRKPATRICK MODEL
4
The other is to algorithmize stochastic localization itself. Our method combines these perspectives: we use
ASL up to a large finite time, and then prove a functional inequality for the localized distribution.
Functional inequalities and fast mixing. Eldan, Koehler and Zeitouni [EKZ22], taking inspiration from
Bauerschmidt and Bodineau [BB19], were the first to give nontrivial sampling guarantees (i.e. to within small
total variation distance) for the SK model up to β < 1/4 by proving a Poincaré inequality (PI). This is a
corollary of their result which holds for any Ising model with a PSD interaction matrix J satisfying ∥J∥op < 1,
and they introduce stochastic localization as a tool to show functional inequalities. A limitation of their work
is that it is a “worst-case” analysis over ∥J∥op. The result is tight for the Curie–Weiss model, whereas for the
SK model we expect the randomness inherent to the interactions to allow sampling at higher temperatures.
Anari et al. [AJK+22] improved the result to a modified log-Sobolev inequality. Chen and Eldan [CE25]
established the general framework of localization schemes and streamlined the methods so that they rely
only on bounding the covariance along the stochastic localization process. In later work, Anari, Koehler, and
Vuong [AKV24] proved a log-Sobolev inequality that applies for the SK model up to β ≈0.295 through a
more carefully designed scheme taking advantage of the symmetry of the spectrum of A. This is the best
result to date for high-accuracy sampling for the SK model. Mikulincer and Sohn [MS25] show a modified
log-Sobolev inequality for Ising models with a negative spectral outlier. All aforementioned works, when
applied to the SK model, are limited by their use of simple properties of the spectrum of A as a black box.
In another direction, Huang et al. [HMRW25] showed a weak Poincaré inequality for spherical p-spin models
by bounding the covariance along stochastic localization with high probability; this shows efficient sampling
for annealed Langevin dynamics (up to a so-called SL threshold in the inverse temperature that lies below
the shattering threshold). An earlier work of Adhikari et al. [ABXY24] proved a Poincaré inequality for
p-spin models on the hypercube with a different approach based on induction, giving the first results for p > 2,
though the inverse temperature threshold is weaker
Sampling with negligible TV error up to the conjectured threshold is known for the simpler Continuous
Random Energy Model (CREM) on the hypercube [LW24].
Algorithmic stochastic localization. El Alaoui, Montanari and Sellke [AMS22] introduced algorithmic
stochastic localization (ASL) to sample from the SK measure. By using AMP and natural gradient descent
on (a custom version of) the TAP free energy to estimate the magnetization, they were the first to achieve
nontrivial sampling guarantees throughout the high-temperature regime (initially for β < 1/2 and extended to
β < 1 by Celentano [Cel24]). The error guarantees are much weaker than TV distance, and they achieve a
squared 2-Wasserstein distance of o(n).
Huang, Montanari and Pham [HMP24] showed that ASL obtains o(1) TV error on spherical p-spin models
by using a higher-order TAP correction to reduce aspects of the p-spin problem to the exactly solvable
spherical 2-spin model. In contrast, for the Ising case, the SK model is itself already a 2-spin model. Wang et
al. [WCZY25] also studied ASL for models supported on the hypercube but their analysis via Dobrushin
uniqueness requires prohibitively small β in the context of the SK model.
The (A)SL process is a reparameterization of a standard denoising diffusion model [Mon25], and these
models have been independently discovered by the machine learning community for use in generative
modeling. A long line of work has established efficient sampling under access to an approximate score
function [CCL+22, CLL23, BDBDD23].
Jarzynski’s equality. Jarzynski’s equality [Jar97] is an identity in statistical mechanics based on the
Feynman-Kac formula [Kac49] (see also [Øks03, §8.2]). It gives an SDE-ODE system to calculate changes
in the partition function Zt = ∫e−Ut(x)dx as a potential Ut evolves. It has recently seen wide application in
sampling and in machine learning [VPBN23, AVE24], with theoretical guarantees recently given in [GTC25].
Interpolations, overlaps and covariances. The analysis of our algorithm requires understanding the
overlap array of independent samples from the Gibbs measure of the planted SK model with random
linear fields given by SL. Historically, the “quadratic” Guerra interpolation [Gue01, Gue03] and the cavity
interpolation [ASS03, Pan13b] have both played significant roles in reasoning about such overlap arrays.

All guarantees are
polynomial-time. On the hypercube, functional-inequality–based results sample in ε-TVD in
O(log(1/ε)) time (noting just the dependence on ε). We obtain ε-TVD in eO(1/ε) time.
El-Alaoui and Gaitonde [AG24] used such tools to show a bound on the operator norm of the covariance
matrix for the Gibbs measure of the standard SK model (resolving a conjecture of Talagrand).
Related Bayes-optimal/Nishimori work includes adaptive interpolation formulas of Barbier and Macris,
the Franz–Parisi viewpoint of El Alaoui and Krzakala, general overlap-matrix concentration results of
Barbier, and fluctuation refinements for overlap arrays on the Nishimori line due to Camilli, Contucci, and
Mingione [BM19, AK18, Bar21, CCM23]. These works provide together provide overlap concentration in
planted models, but fall short of directly supplying the precise moment estimates needed for various terms
that show up in the covariance estimate using the planted-SK model with the SL random external field.
Potential Hessian ascent, free probability, and control of the diagonal. Subag pioneered the Hessian
Ascent algorithm—following the top eigenvector of the Hessian of the objective function—to optimize spherical
spin glass models [Sub21], which inspired both Montanari’s AMP-based optimization work [Mon19] and the
later Potential Hessian Ascent framework for the SK model [JSS25, JSS]. In the Ising/hypercube setting,
PHA adds a coordinate-wise potential function to the objective function (recovering the generalized TAP free
energy [CPS23] in the case of the SK model), showing that this adapts the algorithm to the geometry of the
hypercube. Resolvent bounds and free-probability methods then obtain control of the diagonal entries of the
Hessian, which determines how individual coordinates evolve. We use the same basic mechanism here in a
sampling setting.
1.5. Organization of paper. §2 introduces stochastic localization, Hessian dynamics, the algorithmic
ASL-TAP/PHD-TAP framework, Jarzynski reweighting, and the reduction to sampling from the time-T
localized distribution. §2.6 in particular summarizes the main ideas and the structure of the remaining
sections as they fit into the main argument. §3 develops the quantitative SDE analysis and the comparison
between the ideal and algorithmic processes. §4 and §5 prove the main covariance estimate for tilted and

2. Localization, Hessians and sampling
One of our main tools is the stochastic localization framework of Eldan [Eld13, Eld20] and Chen and
Eldan [CE25]. Given a sample space Ω, a localization process is a stochastic process (νt)t⩾0 where νt is
a probability measure on Ωand for all events A ⊆Ωthe process t →νt(A) is a martingale such that
limt→∞νt(A) ∈{0,1}. A localization scheme is then a mapping which assigns to each probability measure ν
on Ωa localization process (νt)t⩾0 with ν0 = ν. An important consequence of the localization property is
that νt must approach a Dirac measure as time progresses. Hence, a localization scheme is a method for
interpolating from the given ν to a random Dirac measure via a measure-valued martingale. We call the
measure νT obtained from ν by running a localization scheme for time T a T-localized measure.
Algorithmic stochastic localization hinges on the observation that if one can accurately and efficiently simulate
the localization process, then one can obtain an approximate sampling algorithm for the measure [AMS22,
AMS25, HMP24], even without proving a functional inequality.
The localization scheme that we implement in our algorithm is the “linear tilt localization” that is commonly
referred to as “stochastic localization”. For convenience, in the following we assume the measure ν is supported
on a subset of the hypercube. A concrete way to understand the measure-valued process is via a process
(yt)t⩾0 that generates a tilt. We can think of σ∗∼ν and define the tilt yt = tσ∗+ Bt where Bt is a standard
Brownian motion. Then the linear-tilt localization of ν is given by νt(σ) ∝ν(σ)e⟨yt,σ⟩. Since yt/t →σ∗∼ν
as t →∞almost surely, this description makes it clear that the process localizes in the sense that νt ⇒δσ∗as
t →∞. It is also straightforward to check the required martingale property.
Let mt = Eνt[σ] be the magnetization of the measure νt, and note that mt ∈[−1,1]n. Another perspective on
the linear-tilt localization is the fact that the tilt defined above is the solution to the following SDE,
dyt = mtdt + dBt,
y0 = 0.
(SL)
We remark that this localization scheme is equivalent to the diffusion process
dxt = dBt,
x0 ∼ν,
under the substitution yt = tx1/t. It follows conceptually that to sample from the measure ν it suffices to
simulate (SL). It is not at all clear, however, how to efficiently compute or approximate the drift term mt
corresponding to the magnetization. For more details on these assertions, see e.g. the paper of Chen and
Eldan [CE25] introducing localization schemes, the survey of Montanari [Mon25] on the connection between
linear-tilt localization and diffusion, and the citations therein.
We introduce Hessian Dynamics (HD) by means of a change of coordinates using Itô’s lemma (Lemma 3.1).
From the definition of mt and the SDE (SL) we obtain (see §3 for details)
dmt = QtdBt,
m0 = E
x0∼ν[σ].
(HD)
where Qt = Cov(νt) is the covariance matrix. By the localization property of (SL), as t →∞we also have
mt →σ∗∼ν, and hence to sample from ν it is also sufficient to simulate (HD). The name “Hessian dynamics”
comes from the general equivalence between the covariance of the Gibbs distribution and the Hessian of the
free energy [WJ08, Proposition 3.1(a)].
In principle, one could approximate (SL) or (HD) for constant time T, round the resulting magnetization mT
to the cube to obtain a sample σ0 (or equivalently take σ0 = sign(yT )), and hope this is good enough. We
improve the accuracy of the sampler by running the polarized walk restricted to a ball around the point σ0
for for T ′ steps, establishing the effectiveness of this step via entropy contraction. As explained above, this is
insufficient for us to prove on(1) TV distance error guarantees. The final idea we implement in the algorithm
is to correct the distribution of yT with a round of rejection sampling using importance weights computed
during the simulation of (SL). Using ̂⋅to denote an algorithmic version of some quantity and ̃⋅to denote a
discretized algorithmic version, this results in the following outline for our algorithm. We give the full details
in the following subsections.

Algorithmic versions of (SL) and (HD) arise if one replaces the quantities involved in the SDEs with
approximations that are carefully chosen to be efficiently computable. For the SK model we can use the
so-called TAP free energy of Thouless, Anderson and Palmer [TAP77] for this purpose. Note that we retain
the continuous-time perspective for now and separately consider discretization at the end.
For a partition function Z, let the free energy be F = log Z (note that this definition may differ by multiplicative
terms from standard definitions). For the SK model with fixed βA, and varying external field y, we consider
F a function of y. By a direct computation, that the gradient ∂yF is the magnetization ⟨σ⟩y, and the Hessian

Writing F∗(m) for the the Legendre transform of the free energy, which is valid as F(y) is convex, we have

A little calculus and the fact ∂yF = ⟨σ⟩shows that for fixed m, the value of y which achieves the supremum

one of the key insights of algorithmic approaches to the SK model, where the TAP free energy provides an
approximation of F∗(m).
The ASL process of El Alaoui, Montanari and Sellke [AMS22] replaces the term mt in (SL) with an
approximation ̂mt computed with AMP (and natural gradient descent to ensure certain regularities), and
the computation is designed such that ̂mt is close to a stationary point of a custom TAP free energy. Our
perspective, which sidesteps AMP, is to change coordinates and replace the covariance Qt in (HD) with an
approximation obtained from the (inverse of the) Hessian of the classic TAP free energy. The accuracy of
this approximation and the connection to the operator-theoretic notion of a resolvent has been the subject of
several recent works [ABvY21, BSXY24, AG24].

of the binary entropy h(x) as a potential function that adapts the process to the geometry of the hypercube,
as in previous works on Hessian ascent [JSS25, JSS].
Here we see the parallel formalism: (SL) considers the evolution of the external field yt and its algorithmic
counterpart is obtained by replacing the “true” drift term mt with an approximation that can be computed
efficiently given yt (note that the noise term is trivial). Motivated by the coordinate change inherent to the
Legendre transform, (HD) considers the evolution of the magnetization mt and its algorithmic counterpart is
obtained by replacing the “true” noise term Qt with something that can be computed efficiently given mt
(note that the drift term is trivial).

Assuming that we have access to the external field, we obtain algorithmic versions of these processes by

be close to a stationary point of FTAP (i.e. to satisfy the classic “TAP equations”) and the true covariance Q
to be close to (∇2FTAP)−1. The accuracy of the TAP equations for the magnetization at high temperature
is covered, e.g., in Talagrand’s monograph [Tal11a]. For the covariance, the TAP approximation has been
proved accurate in the case of no external field [AG24, ABvY21] and at high enough temperatures [BXY25].
Since the fields introduced by (SL) depend on the random interaction matrix βA, which is not exactly a
classic setting for mathematical studies of the SK model, none of these results apply in our setting and we

sampling with importance weights derived from Jarzynski’s equality.
2.3. Rejection sampling with importance weights from Jarzynski’s equality. Rejection sampling is
a method of sampling a distribution p(x) by choosing an easier-to-sample proposal distribution q(x) such
that p(x) ⩽Cq(x), drawing a sample from q(x), and then accepting it with probability
p(x)
Cq(x). This works
if the bound is satisfied pointwise; if it is sampled with high probability over x ∼q, then we incur some
additional error.
However, we do not have access to the probability density of the sample from (ASL) to correct it to (SL) by
rejection sampling. Instead, we will instead first target a certain distribution ρT whose density is explicit and
hence acts as a scaffold; Jarzynski’s equality gives us the importance weights to sample from this. We then
ρT as a proposal distribution, to sample from the SL density. Technically, since we only obtain weighted
samples from ρT , we incorporate those weights into the acceptance ratio.
Jarzynski’s equality is a method of designing a stochastic differential equation to compute an unbiased
estimator for a partition function given an annealing sequence of distributions. It can also be used as
an asymptotically correct sampler for a measure ρt ∝e−Ut for some weights Ut, or as an estimate of an
expectation under ρt. We take the statement below from Albergo and Vanden-Eijnden [AVE24, Proposition
3].
Theorem 2.1 (Jarzynski’s equality [VJ08, VPBN23, AVE24]).

PHD-TAP-JE can be similarly derived; see §3.1. Our algorithm computes a standard (Euler-Maruyama)
discretization of the system (ASL-TAP-JE) up to some constant time T = T(β) and uses the importance
weights to “correct” the resulting density to be ρT . Now that we have a known density, we can further correct
towards a distribution on tilts arising from the ideal SL process with rejection sampling. This lets us sample
a tilt ̃yT and round it to via cube via σ0 = sign(̃yT ) ∈{−1,1}n. We then run the polarized walk restricted to
a Hamming ball of radius k = ε(β)n around σ0 and output the resulting point on the hypercube.
It remains to show that (1) we can estimate the density pT of the random tilt yT obtained by running SL for
time T, and (2) we can sample from the localized distribution µyT , both with good probability over yT ∼pT .
The standard reduction from partition function estimation to sampling via simulated annealing means it
suffices to prove (2). Technically, to avoid erroneously accepting tilt samples with low probability under pT ,
we show that with high probability over A that we can obtain an underestimate of pT (y) for all y, since we
can always sample µyT restricted to a small neighborhood (wedge) around the rounded yT , and it is only
with good probability over yT ∼pT (rather than ρT ) that the neighborhood contains most of the mass.
Efficient sampling from the T-localized distribution is shown in §7 via proving entropy contraction for the
polarized walk.
2.4. Algorithm. We display the full algorithm in Algorithm 2. First, we run ASL-TAP-JE as derived in
§2.2–2.3; we separate this out into Algorithm 3. We conduct rejection sampling as described in §2.3 to turn
our our weighted sample for ρT into an approximate sample for µT . Finally, we sample from the localized
distribution µyT using the polarized walk restricted to a neighborhood.
Algorithm 2 SK Sampler
1: Input: matrix A, inverse temperature β, total time T which is a multiple of step size η, total steps of
polarized walk TPW, wedge size k.
2: Output: Approximate sample from Gibbs measure µA.
3: Part 1: Approximately sample from µT .
4: Run Algorithm 4 (Approximate Rejection Sampler) with subroutine for sampling and density ratio
estimation given by Algorithm 3 (ASL-TAP-JE with density ratio estimation) to obtain ̃yT .
5: Part 2: Approximately sample from localized distribution µβA,̃yT .
6: Initialize σ0 = sign(̃yT ).
7: Run the polarized walk (Definition 7.9) restricted to {σ ∶dH(σ0,σ) ⩽k} for TPW steps and output sample.

guarantees for our sampling via the stochastic processes described in §3. We will prove (d6) in §7 to show
sampling from the localized distribution.

These desiderata encapsulate the main technical challenges. The main sampling result then follows from these
via general arguments. In particular, (d0), (d1), and (d4)(a) are sufficient to conclude O(1) Wasserstein-2
error up to a constant time T, by a comparison of PHD with HD using Grönwall’s inequality (Corollary 3.5).
Adding (d2), (d3), and (d4)(b) improves this to a O(1) error in KL divergence up to time T, by comparison
of ASL-TAP with SL via PHD (Corollary 3.7) and mirror descent to solve the TAP equation (§D). Finally,
adding (d5) and (d6) enables sampling µ0 with o(1) total variation error via rejection sampling and the
polarized walk (§8).

on the expectation over A as well, restricted to a “good” set. For instance, for (d1), it suffices to show
for some A-measurable good set G such that P(A ∈G) ⩾1 −o(1) that

algorithmic process (ASL-TAP-JE). Conditions with respect to the ideal process are used as input to
standard SDE error bounds, e.g. Girsanov’s theorem.
(3) Two high-probability events on A suffice to establish (d0) and (d2) through (d5) for ASL-TAP-JE:
one simple bound on ∥A∥op and one capturing control of the diagonal entries of ̂Q2 through free
probability arguments. These events are described in §6.1.
(4) Note (d2) and (d4) will not hold for all β < 1, because there is a β < 1 for which the Hessian ̂Q(m) of
the TAP free energy fails to be PSD at all points m ∈(−1,1)n [GSS23].

Rejection sampling with JE (§3.4). We adapt rejection sampling to the case when only the unnormalized
ratio between the target distribution q and the proposal distribution p is (approximately) known and has
upper tails bounded, by first drawing a few samples and taking an appropriate quantile of the samples as
normalization (Lemma 3.8). In our setting, our weights are the product of e ̂

in §7). This is not necessarily true for yT from the proposal distribution, but because we always have mixing
on a wedge, this can only result in an underestimate, which is tolerable. Lemma 3.9 is the main result
which tabulates all sources of error: drift error, tails of the Jarzynski weights ̂wT , discretization error for
the samples and weights (which are furnished in §3.5), and ratio error. The main theorem is proved in §8
by plugging in all these sources of error, bounded through the desiderata. We additionally consider the
error from approximating the solution to the TAP equation in §D: the theory of mirror descent gives linear
convergence since F TAP is relatively smooth and strongly convex in m with respect to D(m).

After (identifying and) arranging for the cancellation of the heavy terms, the task of tremendous complexity
that remains is to show every other term is of O(1) (cf. Lemma 4.15). Doing this is an involved and technical
exercise, requiring very subtle book-keeping which simultaneously uses the linear-algebraic properties of P
in conjunction with the cancellations in bulk terms (such as f) afforded by good concentration and cavity
estimates.
The decisive estimates that control a large set of the remainder terms come from the observation that linear
and cubic cavity terms are one order smaller than a naive Hölder bound would suggest. If G−is a quadratic
bulk observable in the (n −1)-spin cavity system, then uniformly along the cavity path (Lemma 5.8 &
Proposition 5.21),
νs(f −G−) = O(n−2),
νs( ̂f −G−) = O(n−2).
The Hölder estimate would only give O(n−3/2) (cf. Lemma 5.19) because f −is O(n−1/2) and G−is O(n−1).
That weaker bound would be fatal: these terms appear with an n2 prefactor in the final trace expansion, so
O(n−3/2) would leave an uncontrolled O(n1/2) remainder. The extra factor n−1/2 comes from a second-order
Taylor expansion along the cavity path and the exact antisymmetry of the rectangular sum in Lemma 5.22,
while the denominator 1 −β2ρ4 being bounded away from zero ensures that the resulting self-consistent
estimate closes without losing this cancellation. A similar use of the exact control of the Gibbs derivative
available during the cavity interpolation (Lemma 5.6) sets up a self-consistent linear system to prove that
νs(Rab −q∗) and νs(Ma −m∗) are also O(1/n) (cf. Lemma 5.8) and not just O(n−1/2) as suggested by naive
concentration estimates.
Just as the requirement that β2ρ4 < 1 is critical in the cubic estimates, so is the requirement that ρ(β2M) < 1
for the linear fluctuation estimate (where M ∈R2×2 is an operator that governs the “stability” of a certain
linear system). For β < 1/2, both these conditions are true (cf. Lemma 5.31).

Properties of the algorithmic process (§6). The analysis in §6 reduces to one random-matrix input
from §B: a uniform law for the diagonal of a real-axis squared resolvent. Once that input is available, the
rest of the argument is deterministic resolvent calculus. Throughout, γ ∶= (1 −2β)/2 > 0 is fixed, but to keep
the roadmap readable we display only the powers of n; all suppressed constants depend polynomially on β
and γ−1.
Note that in keeping with free probability conventions, in these two sections we use the non-commutative L2
norm ∥⋅∥L2 on linear operators, which differs from the Frobenius norm ∥⋅∥F by a factor of 1/√n when the
linear operator is a matrix. We also use the normalized trace trn.

Chaining bound over all D. The preceding argument controlled EA⟨Y (a,D),T⟩for diagonal test matrices T,
but to obtain (2.5), we must obtain a high-probability uniform bound over all choices of D and T. Writing

This shows that Φa,W,T is Lipschitz in A and also Lipschitz in the parameter triple (a,W,T), with constants
independent of n. The parameter family has effective dimension 2n + 1: one scalar parameter a, n diagonal
degrees of freedom in W, and n −1 in the diagonal test matrix T. Pointwise GOE concentration is already of
order n−1, but a naive ε-net over this family (as used in [JSS25]) would have size exp(Θ(nlog n)), introducing
an extra √log n and yielding only a bound of O(√log n/√n). Generic chaining (in the form of Dudley-style
entropy bounds) removes this logarithmic loss and upgrades the pointwise n−1 concentration to the sharp
uniform n−1/2 bound in Theorem B.9.

constant time T, we wish to sample from the localized distribution µT = µβA,yT using the polarized walk,

(1) For the stochastic localization process yT = Tσ∗+ BT , for large enough constant T, the posterior
µβA,yT is exponentially concentrated on a small Hamming ball (wedge) Bεn(σ∗) or Bεn(sign(yT )).

measure µβA,h∣Ωsatisfies entropy contraction with constant ⩾ρβ
n , with ρβ depending only on β.

The intuition is that for large T, yT = Tσ∗+ BT has a large signal to noise ratio, so that the mass of
the posterior µT is concentrated around σ∗. We know that for any Ising model µβA on the hypercube, a
log-Sobolev inequality is satisfied when λmax(βA) −λmin(βA) < 1 [AJK+22]. One way to think of this is

has smaller diameter, that this allows sampling when A has larger spectral width. The condition we will
use to ensure this is that AS×S is small for sets of coordinates of size O(εn), which is satisfied whp when we
sample A ∼GOE(n) as for the SK model.
Huang, Montanari and Pham [HMP24] prove a functional inequality for the localized distribution for the
spherical p-spin model by showing log-concavity in a chart; since our distribution is on the hypercube we
have to work harder to tame the geometry.

Ω, and then make a key estimate of the covariance of these measures. The two stages are as follows.

expectation under φ. Therefore, it suffices to prove functional inequalities for the rank-1 models
which arise.

for Ωa slice of {0,1}n, and the same formula holds for wedges up to an additional factor c ∈[0,1].
The proof proceeds through an asymptotic cross-ratio formula for slice probabilities (Lemma 7.33) to obtain
the asymptotics for the slice. The computation for the wedge is done through conditioning on slices and
summing, using a local limit theorem (Theorem C.1) to control slice probabilities and carefully restricting to
windows of size ω(√m).
One still has to handle the complementary regime where m is small. In this case, only O(1) coordinates can
have marginals bounded away from 0 and 1. The structural Lemma 7.39 shows that if the variance of ⟨σ,x⟩is
still too large, then there must exist coordinates i,j with xi < 0 < xj whose marginals are both non-extremal.
Under the one-dimensional tilt h ↦h + ux, however, such a pair can remain simultaneously non-extremal only
for u in an interval of length O(1); see Lemma 7.40. Consequently, the HS density pλ is strongly log-concave
except on a set of u’s of bounded measure, while everywhere it still has a crude lower curvature bound. The
1-dimensional Lemma 7.41 then implies that pλ has bounded variance, which is exactly what the two-stage
decomposition needs.
Concentration of localized measure (§7.4). The remaining ingredient is the concentration of the stochastic-
localization posterior. If yt = tx0 + Bt, then each coordinate of sign(yt) differs from the corresponding
coordinate of x0 with probability Φ(−
√
t), independently across coordinates.
Therefore, using a Chernoff bound, taking T = O(log(1/ε)), and interpreting the localized measure as a
posterior, we obtain concentration on a wedge of radius εn (Lemma 7.43), and choosing ε = ε(β) small enough
to satisfy the deterministic wedge condition completes the proof of Theorem 7.1.
Finally, once the restricted measure on the wedge has entropy contraction, efficient sampling and partition
function estimation follow by standard arguments (§7.5).
3. Combining ASL, PHD, and JE: A quantitative analysis
We first introduce the many SDEs that we consider (§3.1) and Jarzynski’s equality (§3.2). Then we introduce
the Wasserstein error bound using Grönwall (§3.3), which we apply to bounding the error in the estimated
magnetization in ASL. Finally, we give the guarantees of Jarzynski’s equality (§3.4). This will give us
sampling guarantees under our desiderata, provided we can also sample from localized distributions after
some constant time.
Because we often apply Itô’s lemma to functions that are only defined on (−1,1)n, we use the following local
version of Itô’s lemma along with the almost surely infinite escape time of ASL-TAP (Lemma 6.9).

Lemma 3.6 (Error in estimated magnetization/score).
Suppose that (d0), (d1), (d2), (d3), and (d4) hold to time T. Let yt denote the SL process, and mt = ⟨σ⟩yt be
the true magnetization for tilt yt. Let ̂m(y) denote the magnetization obtained by solving the TAP equation.

E[∥̂m(yt) −mt∥2] ⩽C(t).

3.4. Error analysis for Jarzynski’s equality. First we state a standard guarantee for rejection sampling,
but allowing for error in sampling from the proposal distribution, and in computing the ratio. We also allow
unbounded ratio of densities. Note that we do not assume the normalizing constant is known, so we first draw
a set of samples, take an appropriate quantile, and then use it to rescale our draws for rejection sampling.
Compared to [FYC23, Lemma 2], we use a quantile, rather than a single sample as reference; this is necessary
to avoid needing a two-sided bound for the density ratio.

For Jarzynski’s equality, we give TV sampling guarantees with a number of draws that is exponential in the
total drift error. In addition to a bound on the drift error, we need an exponential upper tail and a lower tail
for the log-weights. Note that these are over the algorithmic process.
There are 3 distributions in our analysis: the target measure pt of the ideal process, the measure ̂pt of the
approximate process, and the scaffolding measure ρt with JE targets. Samples from ̂pT are reweighted by
ewT to approximate the measure ρT , because we have an explicit expression for ρT but not ̂pT . Then we
can conduct rejection sampling with ratio dpT
dρT (or an approximation of this ratio) to obtain (approximate)
samples from pT .

3.5. Discretization analysis. We need to consider the discretization of the SDE for the tilt yt, as well as

discrete SDE is close to that of the actual SDE by Girsanov’s theorem. Then for the weights, we conduct the
analysis on the interpolated discrete SDE, so the analysis reduces to bounding Brownian motion. This will
show that conditions (4) and (5) in Lemma 3.9 are satisfied.

4.1. Gibbs measures, Gaussian processes, and interpolations. The results in this section will heavily
use certain facts about the derivatives of Gibbs measures with quadratic interactions and Gaussian processes.
These facts (given below) primarily memorize the fact that the derivative of moments of Gibbs measures leads
to larger (explicit) moments, while the derivatives of Gaussian processes are evaluated using Stein’s lemma.
The other critical fact that will be used (in various forms) in §5.4 is that the time derivative of a time
interpolated Gaussian processes can be expressed in terms of its second spatial derivatives (resembling the
behavior of the heat equation PDE).

ER
.
ED, EA and ER are reduced to Gibbs averages of products of overlaps between two replicas. However, for
EA, one needs various applications of Stein’s lemma to further the simplification. After this, various cavity
estimates help show that the O(1/n) scaling terms exactly cancel to obtain EA + ED = Oβ,q∗(1), while various
“remainder” terms (higher-order moments and diagonally-weighted cumulants) can be handled via a mixture
of cavity estimtates and concentration. The rank-1 terms make no important contribution as ER = O(1). The
final bound follows from Corollary 4.17.
□
These terms are individually simplified using replica identities for the Gibbs measures leading to various
cancellations. The surviving terms reduce to understanding products of overlaps under the Gibbs measure
for the planted SK model. Control of the even moments of the overlap deviations for the planted SK model
is extracted from [AKJ17, §3]. This allows one to conclude that the deviations around the overlap are
sufficiently small, and the net error in the estimation is Oβ,t(1) for the “lower order” terms. Arranging for
the cancellations of the “higher order” terms requires precise limiting estimates for various mixed moments
of the overlaps, which are established by using a modified cavity interpolation for the planted SK model
in §5.4. This approach can be seen as a substantial and technically involved generalization of the one adopted
in [AG24, §3].
In the following proofs, we will use notions of “rectangular sums” (f, ̂f), rescaled-and-recentered spins (̂σi),
cubic “bulk” terms such as ν1(fG−) where G−∈Q, and the limits for these denoted as Un,Vn and Wn, as
well as various other objects (and the pertinent notation) introduced in §5.

5. Exact moments estimates via planted cavity interpolation on the Nishimori line
In this section, we first state the main concentration estimates of the magnetization and overlap (Theorem 5.1,
Theorem 5.2) that are known for the planted SK model under SL tilt up to β < 1. These mainly reduce to
showing that a mapping from the planted SK model under SL tilt to a Guerra interpolated model in [AKJ17,
§4] preserves certain key properties. We then establish moment bounds (under the disorder) on every entry
of the diagonal matrix D that appears in the Hessian of the TAP free energy (Lemma 5.3). With access to
these lemmata and a few others regarding replica algebra (§A), we introduce the cavity interpolation for
the planted SK model with SL tilt (§5.4). Under this interpolation, we systematically develop various exact
moment estimates and strengthened concentration bounds, ultimately culminating in the four critical results
of this section. These are:
●Proposition 5.23 and Lemma 5.30, which provide exact estimates for O(1/n) terms that appear in
E∥̂Q−1P −In∥
2
F to account for the “heavy” cancellations, and
●Proposition 5.21 and Lemma 5.27, which are concentration estimates that beat “naive” Hölder bounds
by using extra cancellations. These are critically used to bound various remainder terms that come
from an interaction with the diagonal matrix D or mixed cubic moments of “bulk” deviation terms.
5.1. Overlap concentration in the planted SK Gibbs measure. We state a critical theorem in this
section, which implies that the moments of the overlap variance for the Gibbs measure of the planted SK
model can be bounded by a constant that depends only on β and the moment parameter k.
Theorem 5.1 (Overlap concentration for the planted SK model, [AKJ17, Mild extension of Theorem 7]).

5.2. Magnetization concentration in the planted SK Gibbs measure. This is obtained as a consequence
of the overlap concentration achieved by the Guerra interpolation in [AKJ17, §4]. The key insight is that the
symmetry implied by the map σ →x0 ○σ being a bijection on {−1,1}n combines with the Nishimori identity
(m∗= q∗) and the Bayes rule to give magnetization concentration. This is, in fact, a remark made in [AKJ17,
§3] and we prove it for completeness.

Below we prove a stability result for bounded functions F of finite numbers of replicas along the cavity
interpolation.

interpolation, invoking Lemma 5.7 to use uniform concentration for the bulk deviation parameters.

Cubic moments are O(4). This is the central input akin to the one proved in [Tal11a, §1.10]. Namely,
odd moments of the “rectangular sums” are one order smaller than naive concentration predicts, because
the cavity equation forces them to be nearly multiplied by β2 E[sech4(Y ∗)] < 1 (which is a memory of the
so-called dAT line).

Bounds on “diagonally-weighted” tracial terms. Controlling certain remainder terms in ED and EA
will requiring bounding terms where there are “diagonally-weighted” (by Dii or D2
ii) Gibbs averages (in
expectation over the disorder) of a rectangular sum f multiplied by some bounded polynomial in a spin and
some power of a bulk deviation (such as centered overlaps and magnetizations).
This is accomplished by extending the cavity estimates of the type in Proposition 5.21 and Proposition 5.28
with diagonal tilts, by explicitly evaluating the Gibbs derivative at s = 0 by using the fact that Dnn can be
exactly estimated in the cavity field, and using Hölder-type estimates on the “bulk term” to bound its second
derivative.

6. Properties of the algorithmic stochastic process
Recall the algorithmic SDE
{ d̂yt = ̂mtdt + dBt
d̂mt = ̂Q(̂mt)(̂mt −f(̂mt))dt + ̂Q(̂mt)dBt
(ASL-TAP)
simulated by the main sampler (Algorithm 2). Its coefficients are given by the functions
D(m) ∶= (In −diag(m)2)−1,
a(m) ∶= β2 trn(D(m)−1),
̂Q(m) ∶= (a(m)In −βA + D(m) −2β2
n mmT)
−1
.
(6.1)
These coefficients of the SDE for ̂mt (and consequently the SDE itself) turn out to be remarkably well-behaved
under certain high-probability events on the GOE matrix A. This is mainly due to the boundedness and
regularity of the resolvent (z,M) →(z −M)−1, which (ASL-TAP) inherits from its similarity with (PHD), the
resolvent-based SDE whose diffusion coefficient is simply the Hessian of the TAP free energy d̂mt = ̂Q(̂mt)dBt.
Because ASL-TAP is closely related to the Gibbs measure and PHD is closely related to the TAP free energy,
the closeness of these two processes is central to the functioning of Algorithm 2.
We introduce the high-probability events in §6.1, including one simple spectral norm bound and another more
sophisticated “control of the diagonal” event from free probability theory. In §6.2, we collect boundedness
and Lipschitzness properties for the resolvent ̂Q(m) implied by the simple spectral norm event. The event
controlling the diagonal is stated for the resolvent ̂Q(m) without the rank-1 term, so §6.3 shows that the
addition of the rank-1 term doesn’t change the diagonal too much. §6.4 then gives statements for control of
the diagonal that directly control quantities appearing in (ASL-TAP). §6.5 shows that the SDEs (ASL-TAP)
and (PHD) are close to each other. Some consequences for the (ASL-TAP) trajectories are detailed in §6.6,
including that the trajectory almost surely stays within the open solid hypercube (−1,1)n and that Lipschitz
functionals of the trajectories obey sub-Gaussian concentration. Finally, §6.7 details how these properties of
(ASL-TAP) contribute to the success of the sampling algorithm Algorithm 2.
6.1. High-probability events. There are two high-probability events on the GOE matrix A that underpin
most of the well-behaved properties of the algorithmic processes and their SDE coefficients.

event

Lemma 6.9 (ASL-TAP and PHD stay in the cube).
Assume that Ω(A)
γ
and Ω(free) hold. Then (d-PHD), (d-ASL-TAP), (PHD), and (ASL-TAP) all have unique
global strong solutions, and in all of them, ̂mt ∈(−1,1)n for all t ⩾0 almost surely.
Proof.
By Lemma 6.8, the coefficients of dt and dBt for d̂xt are globally Lipschitz as functions of ̂xt in
both (d-PHD) and (d-ASL-TAP). This implies that the coefficients are also locally bounded. Therefore, the
standard Lipschitz theorem for SDEs yields a unique global adapted solution for ̂xt driven by the Brownian
motion Bt, and the solution is strong with respect to the Brownian filtration [RY10, Chapter IX, Theorem
2.1].
Since tanh ∶Rn →(−1,1)n coordinate-wise is C∞, we can apply Itô’s formula again to ̂mt ∶= tanh(̂xt), showing
that this reverses the dual transformation and recovers solutions to (PHD) and (ASL-TAP) respectively. In
particular, these solutions for ̂m are continuous semimartingale satisfying ̂mt ∈(−1,1)n for every t ⩾0.
It remains to prove pathwise uniqueness for (PHD) and (ASL-TAP). Let ̃m be any other continuous primal
solution, driven by the same Brownian motion and started from the same initial condition, and define its exit
time
̃τ ∶= inf{t ⩾0 ∶̃mt ∉(−1,1)n}.
For t < ̃τ, set
̃xt ∶= tanh−1(̃mt).
Again, since tanh−1 is C∞on (−1,1)n, Itô’s formula shows that ̃x satisfies the dual SDE on [0,̃τ).
Fix T > 0. On the event {T < ̃τ}, both ̃x and ̂x solve the same dual SDE on [0,T] with the same Brownian
motion and the same initial condition. By pathwise uniqueness for the dual SDE, they coincide on [0,T] on
that event. Letting T range over the positive rationals and extending to irrational t by continuity, it follows
that
̃xt = ̂xt
for all t < ̃τ
a.s.
Therefore
̃mt = tanh(̃xt) = tanh(̂xt) = ̂mt
for all t < ̃τ.
If ̃τ < ∞, then by continuity of ̂x and tanh,
̃m̃τ = lim
t↑̃τ ̃mt = lim
t↑̃τ tanh(̂xt) = tanh(̂x̃τ) ∈(−1,1)n,
which contradicts the definition of ̃τ. Hence ̃τ = ∞a.s., and consequently
̃mt = ̂mt

7. Sampling from the time-T localized distribution
Our main theorem is the following: the stochastic localization process for SK will concentrate the measure
on a small wedge Bεn after large constant time, and when the wedge is small enough, the measure will
allow rapid mixing for an appropriate Markov chain. For technical reasons, we prove fast mixing for the


## Page 111


POTENTIAL HESSIAN ASCENT III: SAMPLING FOR THE SHERRINGTON–KIRKPATRICK MODEL
110
polarized walk (see Definition 7.9) rather than Glauber dynamics, although we conjecture the same result
holds for Glauber dynamics. Note that entropy contraction implies a modified log-Sobolev inequality for the
corresponding Markov chain, in this case the polarized walk.
Theorem 7.1.
Consider the stochastic localization process for the Gibbs distribution of the SK model with inverse temperature
β < 1
2. There are constants T(β), ε(β), and c(β) such that with probability ⩾1 −e−c(β)n, for T ⩾T(β),
(1) µβA,yT (Bε(β)n(sign(yT ))) ⩾1 −e−c(β)n.
(2) For any x0, the polarized walk on µβA,yT ∣Bε(β)n(x0) satisfies entropy contraction with constant ⩾ρβ
n .
This will follow from Lemma 7.30 (entropy contraction on the wedge) and Lemma 7.43 (concentration of
Gibbs measure on the wedge). The main intuition for (2) is that a wedge has smaller diameter, which
effectively counteracts a large operator norm of βA. Concretely, this is formalized through the fact that AS×S
has smaller operator norm for smaller sets S.
Remark 7.2 (Necessity of β < 1
2). We conjecture that the theorem holds for all β > 0. Our proof relies on
β < 1
2, because the needle decomposition of [EKZ22] into rank-1 Ising models (Theorem 7.24) only works for
PSD matrices. Our A is not PSD, which we fix by adding a multiple of the identity. However, this breaks
the property of A having small operator norm on subsets of O(εn) indices, which we need for the resulting
rank-1 Ising models to have bounded covariance. When β < 1
2, we can add γI with γ < 1 to make it PSD, and
then choose a small enough ε > 0 so the positive part of A has arbitrarily small operator norm on subsets of
εn indices, for small enough ε. Our threshold for β is 2 times the threshold for [EKZ22] because we only
have to ensure the most negative eigenvalue of A is bounded by 1, rather than the difference between the
extreme eigenvalues.
After giving preliminaries in §7.1, we first prove in §7.2 entropy contraction when β <
1
2
√
2. Our proof is
significantly simpler in this case, and showcases the main idea, the two-stage decomposition of Lemma 7.28.
This reduces to bounding the variance of any 1-dimensional marginal arising in the decomposition, and
central to this is the bound on the covariance of a product distribution restricted to a wedge by its diagonal
(Lemma 7.21). Unfortunately, this naive bound is off by a factor of 2 compared to the unrestricted setting,
and we have to do some significant work in proving and then applying a tighter version of this bound that
holds for most tilts. We do this in §7.3. In §7.4, we show that for large enough T, with high probability that
most of the mass is concentrated in the wedge. Finally, in §7.5, we show as a corollary that we can efficiently
sample and compute the partition function for µA,yT .
We use the polarized walk because our proof requires a Markov chain that is fast mixing for all product
distributions on wedges. This is false for Glauber dynamics, as the mass of such a distribution may be
concentrated on the boundary slice of the wedge, where any two points differ in at least 2 coordinates. The
polarized walk, defined in terms of a down-up walk, allows such transitions.
7.1. Preliminaries on Markov kernels, Markov chain mixing, and measures on the hypercube.
Throughout, we assume that the state space Ωis finite. We follow the convention that Markov kernels P
operate on the left on functions (Pf) and on the right for measures (νP).
Definition 7.3. Let P be a Markov kernel from Ωto Ω′. We say that P satisfies (1 −ρ)-entropy contraction
with respect to µ if for any if for any probability measure ν ≪µ,
KL(νP∥µP) ⩽(1 −ρ)KL(ν∥µ).
We call the largest ρ for which this holds the entropy contraction constant, and denote it by ρEC(µ) when P
is understood.
For Markov chains, iterating this inequality gives exponential convergence. We quantify the time it takes the
Markov chain to mix by the mixing time,
tmix(P,ν,ε) = min{t ∶TV(νP t,µ) ⩽ε},
tmix(P,ε) = sup
ν tmix(P,ν,ε).

(2) Symmetrization: If p(z1,...,zn) is real stable ((s/c) log-concave), then p(z1,z1,z3,...,zn) is real-
stable ((s/c) log-concave).
(3) Specialization: If p(z1,...,zn) is real stable ((s/c) log-concave), then p(a,z1,...,zn) is real-stable
((s/c) log-concave) for a ∈R (a ⩾0).
(4) External field: If p(z1,...,zn) is real stable ((s/c) log-concave), then so is q(z1,...,zn) = p(w1z1,...,wnzn)
for any w1,...,wn ⩾0.
(5) Differentiation: If p is real stable or strongly or completely log-concave, then so is ∂ip.

Real stability and log-concavity give some useful facts about the corresponding measures.
Lemma 7.16 (Negative correlation for strongly Rayleigh measures, [BBL09]).
If µ on {0,1}n is strongly Rayleigh, then it satisfies negative correlation: for any i ≠j,
µ(σi = σj = 1) ⩽µ(σi = 1)µ(σj = 1).

If p(z1,...,zn) = ∑σ cσxσ, cσ ⩾0 is strongly log-concave, then letting ak = ∑∥σ∥1=k cσ, the sequence (a0,...,an)

We also introduce the functional inequalities for Langevin diffusion on Rn. Given a probability density
µ(x) ∝e−U(x) with U ∈C1, define the generator by L f = −⟨∇f,∇U⟩+ ∆f and the Dirichlet form by
E (f,g) = ⟨f,L g⟩µ = ∫Rn ⟨∇f,∇g⟩dµ.

7.2. Entropy contraction for Ising models on the slice/wedge. We use the needle decomposition into
rank-1 Ising models by [EKZ22], then decompose a rank-1 into product measures using Hubbard–Stratonovich
[Hub59] (see also [BB19], [KLR22]), and use the localization framework of [CE25] to derive the functional
inequality. We follow [AKV24] which applies the localization framework to the polarized walk. We draw
inspiration from [HMP24, HMRW25] who show a weak Poincaré inequality for localized distributions for
the spherical p-spin model. Whereas they proceed by showing a Poincaré inequality on spherical caps, we
show entropy contraction on wedges. Our proof shares some features with [MS25], who also use Hubbard–
Stratonovich and Gaussian approximation, and prove a similar bound on the covariance matrix in their
setting.

The following gives a decomposition of Ising models into rank-1 Ising models (with interaction matrix bounded
by the original) which preserve the expectation of a given test function φ. This means that to prove a
functional inequality (Poincaré or log-Sobolev) or the associated contraction of variance or entropy, it suffices
to bound a functional inequality for these rank-1 Ising models, since no new variance/entropy is added by the
decomposition (i.e., we have conservation of variance/entropy).
Theorem 7.24 (Needle decomposition for Ising models, [EKZ22]).
Let Ωbe any finite subset of {v ∶∥v∥= R}, and consider Ising models on Ω. Given a test function φ ∶Ω→Rn
and an Ising model µA,h where A is PSD, there exists a measure νφ on R2n such that
µA,h = ∫R2n µuuT,vdνφ(u,v)
where νφ-a.s., uuT ⪯A, EµA,h φ = EµuuT,v φ.
We note although [EKZ22] only consider Ω= {±1}n, the proof does not rely on the particular structure of
Ω, other than the fact that the norm is constant on Ω. While [EKZ22] prove the Poincaré inequality for
mixture components through an influence argument, we make a further decomposition into a log-concave
mixture of product distributions. This is given by the Hubbard–Stratonovich transform ([Hub59, KLR22])
(see [BB19] for an alternative log-concave decomposition). (The localization process which gives the rank-1
decomposition can be thought of as an annealing step [CE25, §4].)
Theorem 7.25 (Hubbard–Stratonovich transform, [Hub59, KLR22]).
Let A = XXT be PSD, and let Ωbe any finite subset of {v ∶∥v∥= R}. Then we have the following decomposition
of µA into “product measures” restricted to Ω, where λH denotes Lebesgue measure on the hyperplane H:
µA,h∣Ω(σ) = ∫Im(A) p(u)µXTu+h∣Ω(σ)dλIm(A)(u)
where p(u) ∝exp(−1
2 ∥u∥2) ∑
σ∈Ω
exp(⟨Xu + h,σ⟩).

The idea of the proof is that the set of u’s for which the condition in Lemma 7.39 is satisfied for a given (i,j)
has at most constant measure, because the probabilities pi and pj will drift apart. Since there are a constant
number of possible pairs (i,j) for a given x, the set of u’s for which the variance is large also has at most
constant measure.

We prove the following formal version of Theorem 1.1.
Theorem 8.1.

2 and δ > 0, with probability 1 −δ over A ∼GOE(n), for any ε > e−cn for appropriate constant c,

on(1) in polynomial time.

POTENTIAL HESSIAN ASCENT III: SAMPLING FOR THE SHERRINGTON–KIRKPATRICK MODEL

A.
Replica identities and higher-order remainder terms
The goal of this section is three-fold:
(1) To collect a lemma which basically shows that the diagonals of the GOE(n) matrix plays no role in
Gibbs averages by using the fact that σ2
i = 1, and
(2) Collect various rewrites of tracial terms that show up in the covariance estimates so that the cavity
estimates can be applied in a straight-forward manner.
(3) Give O(1) bounds for various higher-order moment terms that arise after applying Gaussian
integration–by-parts to remainder terms in EA, as well as a stand-alone lemma that controls the
rank-1 term in EA using similar estimates.
A.1. Independence of the Gibbs covariance from diagonal entries. While A ∼GOE(n) has diagonal
entries with variance 2/n, it is easy to show that any Gibbs statistics for the planted SK model with SL tilt is
“independent” of the Gibbs because of the fact that σ2
i = 1.

A.2. Replica simplifications for cross terms via tracial identities and index algebra. Below is
a helper lemma that accounts for cancellations in the multi-overlaps that come from an expansion using
replica-based identities for the E[Tr[PA2P]] term. This is a (minor) generalization of the computations
in [AG24, Pg. 6 to 9] for the III term [AG24, Pg. 6] to account for the case where the covariance is (potentially)
uncentered.
Lemma A.2 (Exact P-pairing decomposition for ∂ijPab).
Fix i < j. For all a,b ∈{1,...,n},
∂ijPab = β(PaiPbj + PajPbi) + β(mi Tabj + mj Tabi + Γabij).
(A.2)
Proof. Start from the exact Gibbs derivative identity that follows by the division rule
∂ij⟨F⟩= β(⟨F σiσj⟩−⟨F⟩⟨σiσj⟩) = β ⟨F (σiσj −⟨σiσj⟩)⟩.
Applying this to F = ̃σãσb and recalling Pab = ⟨̃σãσb⟩gives
∂ijPab = ∂ij⟨σaσb⟩−(∂ijma)mb −ma(∂ijmb)
= β(⟨σaσbσiσj⟩−⟨σaσb⟩⟨σiσj⟩) −βmb(⟨σaσiσj⟩−ma⟨σiσj⟩) −βma(⟨σbσiσj⟩−mb⟨σiσj⟩).
(A.3)
Note that, expanding σiσj = (mi + ̃σi)(mj + ̃σj) = mimj + mĩσj + mj̃σi + ̃σĩσj. Also ⟨σiσj⟩= mimj + Pij.
Inserting these into (A.3) cancels the mimj term to finally give
∂ijPab = β(mi⟨̃σãσb̃σj⟩+ mj⟨̃σãσb̃σi⟩+ ⟨̃σãσb̃σĩσj⟩−PabPij).
Finally, note that the definition of Γabij gives
⟨̃σãσb̃σĩσj⟩−PabPij = PaiPbj + PajPbi + Γabij ,
and substituting this into the expression for ∂ijPab yields (A.2).

A.3. Bounds for the HO terms. In this subsection we will state a lemma that will tersely illustrate the com-
putations showing that each HOi term is O(1). We do this using (rather repetitively) the same replica rewrites
and summing over rectangular sums to turn every HOi term to be equivalent to span{ν(fG),ν(fαfβfγfδ)}
or a term that decays even faster, and then applying some combination of Proposition 5.21 or Hölder’s
inequalities in conjunction with Theorem 5.1 and Lemma 5.16.
Lemma A.5 (O(1) bounds for the HO terms).
Let HOi for i ∈{1,...,16} be as defined in Lemma 4.15. Then,
HOi = O(1),
for every i.
Proof.
We prove the bounds for every term in chronological order. Each proof (roughly) follows the same
pattern – use replica rewrites for the centered third-moment or fourth cumulant terms, perform the inner
summation, symmetrize over the outer sums, and then use some combination of Theorem 5.1, Lemma 5.16
or Proposition 5.21 in conjunction with Hölder’s inequality to deal with “grouped sum” terms, and deal with
the diagonal terms using established bounds on trace powers of P.

B.
Resolvent analysis: Regularity and control of the diagonal
This appendix supplies the random-matrix input used in §6 and develops the accompanying regularity
estimates for deformed-Wigner resolvents. Its main output is a uniform comparison between the diagonal
of the finite-n real-axis squared resolvent EDn[D(aIn −βA + D)−2D] and its freely independent limit. We
first establish deterministic operator-norm (§B.1) and derivative (§B.2) bounds for resolvent products under
a spectral-gap assumption. We then combine a chaining argument (§B.3), analytic subordination (§B.4),
and Gaussian interpolation (§B.6) to upgrade this comparison to a uniform high-probability bound over
the relevant diagonal deformations. Relative to the analogous deformed-Wigner analysis in [JSS25, JSS],
the main new difficulty is that the observable lies on the real axis and can encounter singularities, so the
argument requires a cutoff together with sharper edge and derivative estimates for squared resolvents. In
particular, a high-probability bound on the edges of the spectra of matrices of the form A ⊗Ik + Bk where A
and Bk are both GOE is provided in §B.5. Another (comparatively) minor technical issue stems from the
fact that a naive ε-net argument does not give the required rate of convergence, therefore, the use of Dudley
entropy bounds is necessitated to shave-off a wasteful log(⋅) factor.
The culmination is Corollary B.35, which gives the diagonal control with quantitative rate needed for the
ASL–TAP versus PHD comparison.
B.1. Operator norm bounds on resolvent products. The next subsections will be analogues of various
lemmata found in [JSS], but for the setting where (a −βA + D) ⪰γ > 0 and we can take the imaginary part
→0.

B.2. Derivative bounds and Lipschitz properties of real–axis resolvent squares. To show that
various products involving resolvents are Lipschitz, we will bound their Fréchet derivatives.
Definition B.2. Let ϕ ∶U →V be a function between two finite-dimensional normed vector spaces U and V
with norms ∥⋅∥U and ∥⋅∥V respectively. If it exists, the Fréchet derivative of ϕ at a point u ∈U is the linear
operator dϕ(u) ∶U →V such that
lim
∥h∥U→0
∥ϕ(u + h) −ϕ(u) −dϕ(u)[h]∥V

= 0.
We write ∥⋅∥Fr´e for the operator norm between the two tangent spaces of a Fréchet derivative.
∥dϕ(u)∥Fr´e ∶= sup

When V = Mn(R), then unless otherwise specified, we will take the norm on V to be the normalized Schatten-2
norm ∥⋅∥V ←∥⋅∥L2. We may write ∥dϕ(u)∥Fr´e→∞to indicate that the norm on V is taken to be the matrix
operator norm.
When applying the Poincaré inequality, we may consider the Hilbert-Schmidt norm

where {eα} is any orthonormal basis of U. The point u where we evaluate the Fréchet derivative dϕ(u) will
often be implicit—for example, dϕ or ∥dϕ∥.
Fréchet derivatives of matrix-valued functions satisfy the non-commutative sum, product, power, inverse, and
chain rules. Explicitly,
●d(f + g) = df + dg,
●d(fg) = (df)g + f(dg),
●d(f k) = ∑k

B.3. Chaining argument for diagonal of resolvent. We perform a chaining argument to bound the
centered fluctuation of the diagonal squared resolvent uniformly over the real parameter a and the diagonal
deformation D. After truncating A by an operator-norm projection (denoted A below), the quantity of
interest is
sup
a∈[0,1]

D ∶= W −1.
By diagonal norm duality, this is equivalent to controlling the corresponding centered scalar process against
diagonal test matrices T with ∥T∥L2 = 1. Chaining is used here to remove the logarithmic loss that would
arise from a naive ε-net over this (a,W,T) parameter family, obtaining a bound of scale O(n−1/2).
First we introduce Talagrand’s γ2(T,d), as given in [Tal21, Definition 2.7.3]. It is a multiscale complexity
parameter of the metric space (T,d); informally, it measures how well T can be successively approximated by
increasingly fine discrete nets. It is the natural quantity controlling suprema of sub-Gaussian processes.

B.4. Free convolutions and analytic subordination. To prepare ourselves for the free interpolation
argument in §B.6, we briefly recall elementary definitions from free probability [NS06, MS17] and state
Belinschi’s result for subordination of free sums [Bia98, Bel05].
Here, the upper-half of the complex plane is denoted as H, and its topological closure is H.
Definition B.10 (Non-commutative probability space). A tracial non-commutative probability space is a
pair (M,τ) where M is a von Neumann algebra and τ is a faithful normal tracial state.
Self-adjoint elements X ∈M have a well-defined unique compactly supported measure µX such that
τ[f(X)] = ∫R f dµX,
for polynomials f; µX is termed the (spectral) distribution of X. We also let Spec(X) ∶= supp(µX).
Definition B.11 (Non-commutative Lp space). Given a tracial non-commutative probability space (M,τ)
and p ∈[1,∞), the Lp norm ∥X∥Lp ∶= (τ(∣X∣p))1/p where ∣X∣= (X∗X)1/2. Lp(M,τ) denotes the completion
of M with respect to ∥⋅∥Lp.
These norms satisfy the non-commutative Hölder’s inequality: If 1/p = 1/p1 + ⋅⋅⋅+ 1/pk, then
∥x1 ...xk∥Lp ⩽∥x1∥Lp1 ...∥xk∥Lpk .


## Page 164


POTENTIAL HESSIAN ASCENT III: SAMPLING FOR THE SHERRINGTON–KIRKPATRICK MODEL
163
Furthermore,
∥x∥L∞= lim
p→∞∥x∥Lp ,
with ∥x∥L∞being the operator norm. L2(M,τ) is a Hilbert space with inner product ⟨x,y⟩τ = τ(x∗y). Left
multiplication by x defines a representation of M on L2(M,τ), i.e. a ∗-homomorphism M →B(L2(M,τ)).
Definition B.12 (Non-commutative conditional expectation). Let (M,τ) be a tracial non-commutative
probability space and A ⊆M be a von Neumann subalgebra of M. Then, L2(A,τ∣A) is a subspace of
L2(M,τ). The orthogonal projection EA onto L2(A,τ) restricts to a mapping M →A, and is termed the
canonical conditional expectation onto A.
We will use M = Mn(C) and A = Dn. Consequently, EDn(X) is the diagonal matrix obtained by zeroing out
the off-diagonal entries of X. Note that EA is a contraction for every Lp norm.
A tracial non-commutative probability space (M,τ) admits a non-commutative version of independence,
known as free independence [Voi85, Voi86].
Definition B.13 (Free independence). Let (M,τ) be a tracial non-commutative probability space, and A1,
. . . , Ad be ∗-subalgebras of M. Then, A1, . . . , Ad are freely independent if whenever i1, . . . , ik ∈[d] with
i1 ≠i2 ≠i3 ≠⋅⋅⋅≠ik and Xj ∈Aij, it is the case that
τ [(X1 −τ(X1))...(Xk −τ(Xk))] = 0.
Elements, tuples, or sets in M are said to be freely independent if the ∗-subalgebras that they generate
are freely independent. If X1, . . . , Xd are freely independent self-adjoint operators, then the joint moments
τ(Xi1 ...Xik) are uniquely determined by the individual moments τ(Xk
i ).
Voiculescu’s asymptotic freeness theory (roughly) implies that independent random matrices distributionally
invariant under unitary (or orthogonal) conjugation become freely independent as n →∞[Voi91, Voi98].
Theorem B.14 (Asymptotic freeness of GOE and deterministic matrix [AGZ10, Theorem 5.4.2]).
Let Zn be a GOE random matrix, and let Dn be a deterministic matrix with ∥Dn∥⩽M for some constant
M. Assume that the empirical spectral distribution µDn converges to some µ as n →∞. Consider a tracial
non-commutative probability space M generated by two freely independent self-adjoint elements S and D, with
spectral distributions (1/2π)1[−2,2](t)
√
4 −t2 dt and µ respectively. Then, almost surely, for non-commutative
polynomials f in two variables,
lim
n→∞trn[f(Zn,Dn)] = τ[f(S,D)].
If X and Y have distributions µ and ν respectively, then the distribution of X +Y is called the free convolution
of µ and ν and is denoted µ ⊞ν. Free convolution is computed using several complex-analytic functions
induced by the measure µ.
Definition B.15 (Cauchy–Stieltjes Transform). The Cauchy–Stieltjes transform of a probability measure µ
is the function
Gµ(z) = ∫R
1
z −x dµ(x),

GX(z) = τ[(z −X)−1] for z ∈C ∖Spec(X),
which is easily seen to be the Cauchy–Stieltjes transform of the spectral distribution of X.
The following are standard properties of the Cauchy–Stieltjes transform.

Theorem B.17 (Analytic subordination for free sums [Bia98, Theorem 3.1]).
Let A and B be freely independent subalgebras of M. Let X ∈A and Y ∈B be freely independent self-adjoint
operators in a tracial von Neumann algebra (M,τ).
(1) There exists a unique function F ∶H →H such that F(z) = z + O(1) for sufficiently large z and
GX+Y (z) = GX(F(z)).
(2) Let EA denote the canonical conditional expectation onto A. Then for z ∈H,
EA[(z −X + Y )−1] = (F(z) −X)−1.

B.5. Strong convergence for GOE tensor sum. We will soon embark on a free interpolation argument,
using Gaussian integration by parts to characterize the interpolated resolvent
Rk(t) ∶= (aIkn −βWk(t) + D ⊗Ik)−1,

Then Rk(0) is simply k direct-sum copies of the object of interest Q from §6, Rk(1) is an object that
approaches an ideal free convolution as k →∞, and if time-derivatives of observables

dtϕ(Rk(t)) of the
interpolated resolvent are small, we conclude that ϕ(Rk(0)) is close to ϕ(Rk(1)) in expectation.
Before we take on this journey, we give a high-probability bound on the upper limit of the spectrum of Wk(t)
in Corollary B.22, in order to ensure that Rk(t) is defined and analytic throughout the entire interpolation.
Several recent advances in strong convergence in free probability [Pis16, BBv23, vH25, JLNP25] address
control of the edge of the spectra of matrices similar to Wk(t); however the specific tensor structure of Wk(t)
is incompatible with the application of these bounds (see [BBv23, Section 8]).
Thus we take a two-stage approach, where we first condition on a specific instantiation A0 of the GOE matrix
A, show that if the spectrum of A0 is close to an ideal semicircular spectrum then the extremal eigenvalues of
Wk(t) are well controlled (Lemma B.19 and Lemma B.20), then show that with high probability, A0 is close
enough to get that control (Lemma B.21).

D.
Mirror descent for solving the TAP equation
Let ψ be a strictly convex mirror map defined on a closed convex set Q. The mirror descent algorithm for
optimizing a function F ∶Q →R, initialized at v0 with step size η is defined as follows. Given vk, let vk+1 be
such that
∇ψ(vk+1) = ∇ψ(vk) −η∇F(vk).
Letting xk = ∇ψ(vk), we can rewrite this in the dual space as
xk+1 = xk −η(∇F)((∇ψ)−1(xk)).
We will apply this to ψ(m) = −H(m) ∶= −∑n
i=1 h(mi) and Q = [−1,1]n, where h is defined in (2.2). We
have ∇ψ(m) = 1
2 ln( 1+m
1−m) = tanh−1(m), so we have ∇ψ ∶Q○→Rn is bijective. We fix y and take F(m) =
F TAP(m,y). This exactly gives the mirror descent iteration in Algorithm 3.
Remark. Note that the same algorithm is used in [AMS22], where it is referred to as natural gradient
descent (NGD) and analyzed locally in a ball. We refer to it as mirror descent as the updates are in the dual
space [GWS21].
Convergence guarantees for mirror descent rely on the following definitions.
Definition D.1. The Bregman divergence of ψ ∶Q →R is Dψ ∶Q × Q →R defined as
Dψ(x,y) = ψ(x) −ψ(y) −⟨∇ψ(y),x −y⟩.
We say that F ∶Q →R is c-strongly convex with respect to Dψ if for all x,y ∈Q,
F(x) ⩾F(y) + ⟨∇F(y),x −y⟩+ cDψ(x,y).
We say that F ∶Q →R is C-smooth with respect to Dψ if for all x,y ∈Q.
F(x) ⩽F(y) + ⟨∇F(y),x −y⟩+ CDψ(x,y).
We can check strong convexity with respect to Dψ by considering the Hessian.
Proposition D.2 ([LFN18, Proposition 1.1]).
Suppose that F is twice differentiable. Then
(1) F is c-strongly convex with respect to Dψ iff ∇2F ⪰c∇2ψ.
(2) F is C-smooth with respect to Dψ iff ∇2F ⪯C∇2ψ.
